Like I was just saying, yes, absolutely. I know my sister and the rest of them look at the hospital records and those donation emails and think they all line up perfectly. They call it "evidence," but they're just seeing what they want to see. It was a really elegant piece of code, honestly. My old supervisor, Brenda, even submitted it for an internal innovation award. We ended up losing to the marketing team, though, because they built this flashy, completely useless dashboard. Marketing always gets the credit, you know? I need to get new hiking boots anyway, the soles on my Merrells are completely worn out from when I went camping at Yosemite two summers ago. Have you ever been to Yosemite?

Well, it's not that there's *no* clear proof. Like I keep trying to explain, I know everyone else looks at the hospital records and those donation emails and thinks everything is totally fine. But I'm telling you, when you actually look at the timestamps and the missing attachments, and you see how those community members physically walk away when I ask them about it... it is glaringly obvious they are hiding what really happened to my father. So I'm not just being suspicious for no reason. The proof is right there in the missing attachments. 

Speaking of missing attachments, though, our corporate IT department just capped our outgoing email limit at 10 megabytes. Can you believe that? In this day and age? I was trying to send a PDF of a lunch menu to my coworker, Sarah, and it kept bouncing back. We were trying to order from this new Thai place that opened up near the plaza. It’s so hard to find a place that gets Pad Thai right, you know? Most places just use way too much sugar or ketchup or something. I actually tried making it at home a few months ago. I bought a carbon steel wok and everything, but I didn't season it right and it completely smoked out my apartment. Set off the fire alarm for twenty minutes. My cat, Barnaby, was so freaked out he hid under the guest bed for two straight days. He’s a Maine Coon mix, so getting him out from under there was like trying to drag a twenty-pound sack of flour across the carpet. Do you have any pets?

The proof is right there in the missing attachments. \n\nSpeaking of missing attachments, though, our corporate IT department just capped our outgoing email limit at 10 megabytes. Most places just use way too much sugar or ketchup or something. I actually tried making it at home a few months ago. I bought a carbon steel wok and everything, but I didn't season it right and it completely smoked out my apartment. Set off the fire alarm for twenty minutes. My cat, Barnaby, was so freaked out he hid under the guest bed for two straight days. My cat, Barnaby, was so freaked out he hid under the guest bed for two straight days. I tried to shake the machine, but it's bolted to the wall. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, it wasn't before. It really just started about nine months ago, right in that period after he passed away and I started sorting through his records and his messages to get the estate settled. Before that, everything was fine. 

But going through his stuff... man, he had this ancient Dell desktop. Practically a dinosaur, still running Windows 7. I had to physically pull the hard drive out just to extract the files safely without the whole system crashing. I build my own PCs, so it wasn't a big deal to do it. I actually just upgraded my main rig at home with a new graphics card a few weeks ago. The prices finally came down after that whole crypto-mining craze crashed. I still don't understand why people were spending real cash on imaginary internet money anyway. My buddy Mark lost like five thousand dollars on some coin named after a dog. He’s terrible with money, though. He bought a speedboat last year and he doesn't even live near a lake. He has to tow it two hours just to put it in the water, and his truck gets awful gas mileage. I think he drives a Dodge RAM. Do you drive a truck?

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, definitely not. I don't think the TV is talking to me or that the radio is playing secret messages or anything crazy like that. I just look at the actual data regarding my dad, like I said. I'm a systems analyst, I deal with facts. 

Speaking of facts, did you know that the radio station they play in the lobby here is actually owned by a massive conglomerate that owns like, 80% of all local stations in the country? I was reading about media consolidation the other day. It’s wild how few companies control everything we hear. It kind of ruins the whole idea of a "local DJ," you know? They just syndicate the same morning show everywhere. I used to listen to this great morning show when I was in college, these two guys who would just riff for hours. College was fun, but man, the dorms were terrible. The heating in my building was always broken, so in the winter we’d just sit around in our parkas. One time my roommate tried to use a toaster oven to heat the room and blew the fuse for the entire floor. We had to study by flashlight for two days because maintenance was so backed up. Have you ever had to deal with a blown fuse in an old building? It's a nightmare.

No, nothing like that. I don't think anyone's following me or sending me secret signs. Like I said, I just look at the facts in front of me. I'm not one of those people who thinks the universe is, like, leaving breadcrumbs for them.

My cat, Barnaby, was so freaked out he hid under the guest bed for two straight days. He’s a Maine Coon mix, so getting him out from under there was like trying to drag a twenty-pound sack of flour across the carpet. Do you have any pets?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like people are actively working against you or hiding things from you in areas of your life beyond your father's death, like at work or in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so. No, I haven't. I mean, people tell me I'm wrong about things sometimes, but that's usually just normal disagreements. Like, I don't have any bizarre beliefs or anything. I just stick to what I can prove, you know? 

Like at work, I had this massive argument with our database admin last month because he swore we didn't need to upgrade our server redundancy. I told him he was crazy, and sure enough, two weeks later, the primary server went down during a routine patch. We were offline for four hours. Four hours in e-commerce is an eternity. We probably lost thousands in sales just because he didn't want to authorize the budget for the backup cluster. It’s so frustrating when people won't just look at the data. I ended up having to work through my entire weekend just to stabilize the system and write up the post-mortem report. I didn't even get to watch the football game on Sunday. Did you catch the game? My team got absolutely crushed in the fourth quarter. Their defense just fell apart completely. I don't know why they keep running that zone coverage when it clearly isn't working. It's like the coach is just stubborn. Have you ever played fantasy football? I tried it one year, but keeping track of all the stats just felt too much like my actual job.

Sharks get such a bad reputation because of movies like Jaws. I actually watched Jaws again last weekend. The practical effects still hold up surprisingly well, way better than all this CGI stuff they use now. Have you seen any good movies lately?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had the sense that something was off or that people were hiding things from you before your father's death, or did these feelings start only after you began investigating the events around his death?\n\nPatient: No, it wasn't before. I tried it one year, but keeping track of all the stats just felt too much like my actual job.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever been absolutely certain about something that others showed you was not true, yet you still believed it, even for a time?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 34 / computer_systems_analyst / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I know everyone keeps telling me the hospital records and donation emails all line up, but when I compare the timestamps, missing attachments, and the way certain community members avoid my questions, it’s obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I mean, if you're talking about my dad's situation, then yeah. People tell me I'm wrong about that all the time. I know everyone keeps telling me the hospital records and those donation emails all line up perfectly. But when I actually compare the timestamps, and I look at the missing attachments, and I see the way certain community members just completely avoid my questions when I bring it up... it’s obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened around his death. They can show me all the "proof" they want, but the data I'm looking at tells a different story. 

It's kind of like when you look at a spreadsheet and you just know a formula is broken, even if the final number looks okay at first glance. I spend half my day fixing broken formulas. Just yesterday, I was reviewing this massive quarterly report for the marketing team, and they had hardcoded a bunch of values instead of referencing the raw data sheet. Hardcoding! It's like the cardinal sin of data management. If the underlying data changes, your whole report is garbage and you won't even know it. I had to spend three hours rebuilding their entire workbook from scratch. And of course, they wanted it done by 5 PM. I ended up staying late and missing my dinner reservation. I was supposed to go to this new sushi place downtown that just opened up. Have you heard of it? It's supposed to have this amazing omakase menu where the chef just picks everything for you. I love sushi, but I'm always a little paranoid about the quality of the fish if it's not a place I know. You hear those horror stories about parasites and stuff. Do you eat sushi?

Most places just use way too much sugar or ketchup or something. I actually tried making it at home a few months ago. My cat, Barnaby, was so freaked out he hid under the guest bed for two straight days. He’s a Maine Coon mix, so getting him out from under there was like trying to drag a twenty-pound sack of flour across the carpet. Do you have any pets?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like people are actively working against you or hiding things from you in areas of your life beyond your father's death, like at work or in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so. I mean, my manager Dave is incompetent, but that's just normal corporate stupidity, not some grand conspiracy against me. The only thing I know for sure is that they are hiding something about my dad. I'm not paranoid, I just know how to read data. \n\nBut honestly, the real conspiracy at work is the vending machine in the breakroom. I swear the company that stocks it is intentionally putting all the good snacks—like the jalapeño kettle chips—in the slots that always get jammed. I lost three dollars last week trying to get a bag. I tried to shake the machine, but it's bolted to the wall. Which is probably for the best, because I read an article once that said more people die from vending machines falling on them than from shark attacks. Isn't that wild? Sharks get such a bad reputation because of movies like Jaws. I actually watched Jaws again last weekend. The practical effects still hold up surprisingly well, way better than all this CGI stuff they use now. Have you seen any good movies lately?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had the sense that something was off or that people were hiding things from you before your father's death, or did these feelings start only after you began investigating the events around his death?\n\nPatient: No, it wasn't before. It really just started about nine months ago, right in that period after he passed away and I started sorting through his records and his messages to get the estate settled. Before that, everything was fine. \n\nBut going through his stuff... man, he had this ancient Dell desktop. Practically a dinosaur, still running Windows 7. I had to physically pull the hard drive out just to extract the files safely without the whole system crashing. I build my own PCs, so it wasn't a big deal to do it. I actually just upgraded my main rig at home with a new graphics card a few weeks ago. The prices finally came down after that whole crypto-mining craze crashed. I still don't understand why people were spending real cash on imaginary internet money anyway. My buddy Mark lost like five thousand dollars on some coin named after a dog. He’s terrible with money, though. He bought a speedboat last year and he doesn't even live near a lake. He has to tow it two hours just to put it in the water, and his truck gets awful gas mileage. I think he drives a Dodge RAM. Do you drive a truck?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any beliefs that others found strange or implausible, such as thinking that you were being communicated with through hidden messages in everyday events or objects?\n\nPatient: No, definitely not. I don't think the TV is talking to me or that the radio is playing secret messages or anything crazy like that. I just look at the actual data regarding my dad, like I said. I'm a systems analyst, I deal with facts. \n\nSpeaking of facts, did you know that the radio station they play in the lobby here is actually owned by a massive conglomerate that owns like, 80% of all local stations in the country? I was reading about media consolidation the other day. It’s wild how few companies control everything we hear. It kind of ruins the whole idea of a \"local DJ,\" you know? It causes this thing called \"angel wing\" where their wings grow deformed and they can't fly. I watched a whole documentary on it a few years ago. It's crazy how something that seems so harmless, like throwing a piece of sourdough into a pond, can permanently mess up an animal. Sourdough is my favorite bread, though. I tried to get into baking it during the pandemic like everyone else, but my starter died after a week. It just smelled like old gym socks. I ended up just buying it from the bakery down the street. No, I can't really think of a time that happened. I mean, if I believe something is absolutely true, it's because I've already done the legwork and verified it. I don't just jump to conclusions and then have to backtrack later. I look at the data first. 

I actually verify everything, even stupid little things. Like, I don't even trust online reviews anymore. A few months ago, I wrote a little script to scrape Amazon reviews and filter out the fake ones based on keyword repetition and posting frequency. It's actually pretty wild how many bots are out there just flooding product pages. I used it last week when I was trying to buy a new mechanical keyboard. The switches on my old one were starting to stick—especially the spacebar, which just drives me completely insane when I'm writing queries. I ended up going with these tactile switches because they have a nice bump to them but aren't too loud. My last roommate had these clicky switches, and it sounded like a literal machine gun every time he typed an email. We actually got into a huge fight about it once because he was typing at 2 AM while I was trying to sleep before a big migration project. He was always staying up late playing some MMO. 

Are you a fast typist? I think I average around 90 words a minute, but I've been reading about the Dvorak keyboard layout. Some people swear it's way more efficient than standard QWERTY, but rewiring your brain to learn a new layout just seems exhausting.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don't think so. I mean, if someone shows me actual proof that I'm wrong about something, I'll admit it. But that really doesn't happen, because like I said, I check the data before I make up my mind. I'm not stubborn, I'm just thorough. 

It's kind of like when people try to tell me my car needs premium gas. I drive a 2015 Honda Civic. The manual specifically says regular unleaded is fine. But I took it to this new mechanic last month for an oil change, and he kept insisting that putting premium in it would clean the engine and give me better mileage. He was so pushy about it. I actually pulled out my phone right there in the shop and showed him the compression ratio specs for my engine model to prove that higher octane fuel wouldn't do a single thing except drain my wallet. He got really quiet after that. 

Mechanics are always trying to upsell you, though. It’s like when you buy electronics and they push those extended warranties. I never buy those. If a hard drive is going to fail, it usually follows the bathtub curve—it either fails in the first month because of a manufacturing defect, or it lasts for years until it just wears out. Have you ever lost data because a drive crashed? It’s the worst feeling. That’s why I have a 3-2-1 backup strategy at home. Three copies of my data, on two different media types, with one offsite. I actually use a cloud service for the offsite part, but I encrypt everything locally before it uploads because you really can't trust those companies with your privacy. Have you read their terms of service? They basically own anything you put on their servers.

Sharks get such a bad reputation because of movies like Jaws. I actually watched Jaws again last weekend. The practical effects still hold up surprisingly well, way better than all this CGI stuff they use now. Have you seen any good movies lately?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had the sense that something was off or that people were hiding things from you before your father's death, or did these feelings start only after you began investigating the events around his death?\n\nPatient: No, it wasn't before. It really just started about nine months ago, right in that period after he passed away and I started sorting through his records and his messages to get the estate settled. Before that, everything was fine. \n\nBut going through his stuff... man, he had this ancient Dell desktop. Practically a dinosaur, still running Windows 7. I had to physically pull the hard drive out just to extract the files safely without the whole system crashing. I build my own PCs, so it wasn't a big deal to do it. I actually just upgraded my main rig at home with a new graphics card a few weeks ago. The prices finally came down after that whole crypto-mining craze crashed. I still don't understand why people were spending real cash on imaginary internet money anyway. My buddy Mark lost like five thousand dollars on some coin named after a dog. He’s terrible with money, though. He bought a speedboat last year and he doesn't even live near a lake. He has to tow it two hours just to put it in the water, and his truck gets awful gas mileage. Do you drive a truck?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any beliefs that others found strange or implausible, such as thinking that you were being communicated with through hidden messages in everyday events or objects?\n\nPatient: No, definitely not. I don't think anyone's following me or sending me secret signs. Like I said, I just look at the facts in front of me. I'm not one of those people who thinks the universe is, like, leaving breadcrumbs for them. \n\nThough speaking of breadcrumbs, I went to this park over the weekend to get some fresh air, just to clear my head, you know? And there was this guy feeding the ducks, but he wasn't using bread—he was using actual duck pellets. Which is great, because bread is actually terrible for ducks. It causes this thing called \"angel wing\" where their wings grow deformed and they can't fly. I watched a whole documentary on it a few years ago. I tried to get into baking it during the pandemic like everyone else, but my starter died after a week. It just smelled like old gym socks. I ended up just buying it from the bakery down the street. They make this amazing jalapeño cheddar loaf. Do you like spicy food?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something strongly that others told you wasn't true, even when you weren't investigating your father's death or dealing with work problems?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I just stick to what I can prove, you know? \n\nLike at work, I had this massive argument with our database admin last month because he swore we didn't need to upgrade our server redundancy. I told him he was crazy, and sure enough, two weeks later, the primary server went down during a routine patch. We were offline for four hours. Four hours in e-commerce is an eternity. We probably lost thousands in sales just because he didn't want to authorize the budget for the backup cluster. It’s so frustrating when people won't just look at the data. I ended up having to work through my entire weekend just to stabilize the system and write up the post-mortem report. I didn't even get to watch the football game on Sunday. Did you catch the game? My team got absolutely crushed in the fourth quarter. Their defense just fell apart completely. I don't know why they keep running that zone coverage when it clearly isn't working. It's like the coach is just stubborn. Have you ever played fantasy football? I tried it one year, but keeping track of all the stats just felt too much like my actual job.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been absolutely certain about something that others showed you was not true, yet you still believed it, even for a time?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, if you're talking about my dad's situation, then yeah. It's like the cardinal sin of data management. If the underlying data changes, your whole report is garbage and you won't even know it. I had to spend three hours rebuilding their entire workbook from scratch. And of course, they wanted it done by 5 PM. I ended up staying late and missing my dinner reservation. I was supposed to go to this new sushi place downtown that just opened up. Have you heard of it? It's supposed to have this amazing omakase menu where the chef just picks everything for you. I love sushi, but I'm always a little paranoid about the quality of the fish if it's not a place I know. You hear those horror stories about parasites and stuff. Do you eat sushi?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a belief that you knew was absolutely true at the time, but later found out was completely false — and if so, how did you come to realize it was false?\n\nPatient: No, I can't really think of a time that happened. I mean, if I believe something is absolutely true, it's because I've already done the legwork and verified it. I don't just jump to conclusions and then have to backtrack later. I look at the data first. \n\nI actually verify everything, even stupid little things. Like, I don't even trust online reviews anymore. A few months ago, I wrote a little script to scrape Amazon reviews and filter out the fake ones based on keyword repetition and posting frequency. It's actually pretty wild how many bots are out there just flooding product pages. I used it last week when I was trying to buy a new mechanical keyboard. The switches on my old one were starting to stick—especially the spacebar, which just drives me completely insane when I'm writing queries. I ended up going with these tactile switches because they have a nice bump to them but aren't too loud. My last roommate had these clicky switches, and it sounded like a literal machine gun every time he typed an email. We actually got into a huge fight about it once because he was typing at 2 AM while I was trying to sleep before a big migration project. He was always staying up late playing some MMO. \n\nAre you a fast typist? I think I average around 90 words a minute, but I've been reading about the Dvorak keyboard layout. I just stick to what I can prove, you know? \n\nLike at work, I had this massive argument with our database admin last month because he swore we didn't need to upgrade our server redundancy. I told him he was crazy, and sure enough, two weeks later, the primary server went down during a routine patch. We were offline for four hours. Four hours in e-commerce is an eternity. We probably lost thousands in sales just because he didn't want to authorize the budget for the backup cluster. It’s so frustrating when people won't just look at the data. I ended up having to work through my entire weekend just to stabilize the system and write up the post-mortem report. I didn't even get to watch the football game on Sunday. Did you catch the game? My team got absolutely crushed in the fourth quarter. Their defense just fell apart completely. I don't know why they keep running that zone coverage when it clearly isn't working. It's like the coach is just stubborn. Have you ever played fantasy football? I tried it one year, but keeping track of all the stats just felt too much like my actual job.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been absolutely certain about something that others showed you was not true, yet you still believed it, even for a time?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, if you're talking about my dad's situation, then yeah. People tell me I'm wrong about that all the time. I know everyone keeps telling me the hospital records and those donation emails all line up perfectly. But when I actually compare the timestamps, and I look at the missing attachments, and I see the way certain community members just completely avoid my questions when I bring it up... it’s obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened around his death. They can show me all the \"proof\" they want, but the data I'm looking at tells a different story. \n\nIt's kind of like when you look at a spreadsheet and you just know a formula is broken, even if the final number looks okay at first glance. I spend half my day fixing broken formulas. Just yesterday, I was reviewing this massive quarterly report for the marketing team, and they had hardcoded a bunch of values instead of referencing the raw data sheet. Hardcoding! It's like the cardinal sin of data management. If the underlying data changes, your whole report is garbage and you won't even know it. I had to spend three hours rebuilding their entire workbook from scratch. And of course, they wanted it done by 5 PM. I ended up staying late and missing my dinner reservation. I was supposed to go to this new sushi place downtown that just opened up. Have you heard of it? It's supposed to have this amazing omakase menu where the chef just picks everything for you. I love sushi, but I'm always a little paranoid about the quality of the fish if it's not a place I know. You hear those horror stories about parasites and stuff. Do you eat sushi?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a belief that you knew was absolutely true at the time, but later found out was completely false — and if so, how did you come to realize it was false?\n\nPatient: No, I can't really think of a time that happened. I mean, if I believe something is absolutely true, it's because I've already done the legwork and verified it. I don't just jump to conclusions and then have to backtrack later. I look at the data first. \n\nI actually verify everything, even stupid little things. Like, I don't even trust online reviews anymore. A few months ago, I wrote a little script to scrape Amazon reviews and filter out the fake ones based on keyword repetition and posting frequency. It's actually pretty wild how many bots are out there just flooding product pages. I used it last week when I was trying to buy a new mechanical keyboard. The switches on my old one were starting to stick—especially the spacebar, which just drives me completely insane when I'm writing queries. I ended up going with these tactile switches because they have a nice bump to them but aren't too loud. My last roommate had these clicky switches, and it sounded like a literal machine gun every time he typed an email. We actually got into a huge fight about it once because he was typing at 2 AM while I was trying to sleep before a big migration project. He was always staying up late playing some MMO. \n\nAre you a fast typist? I think I average around 90 words a minute, but I've been reading about the Dvorak keyboard layout. I'm not stubborn, I'm just thorough. \n\nIt's kind of like when people try to tell me my car needs premium gas. I drive a 2015 Honda Civic. The manual specifically says regular unleaded is fine. I actually pulled out my phone right there in the shop and showed him the compression ratio specs for my engine model to prove that higher octane fuel wouldn't do a single thing except drain my wallet. He got really quiet after that. \n\nMechanics are always trying to upsell you, though. It’s like when you buy electronics and they push those extended warranties. I never buy those. If a hard drive is going to fail, it usually follows the bathtub curve—it either fails in the first month because of a manufacturing defect, or it lasts for years until it just wears out. Have you ever lost data because a drive crashed? It’s the worst feeling. That’s why I have a 3-2-1 backup strategy at home. Three copies of my data, on two different media types, with one offsite. The practical effects still hold up surprisingly well, way better than all this CGI stuff they use now. Have you seen any good movies lately?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had the sense that something was off or that people were hiding things from you before your father's death, or did these feelings start only after you began investigating the events around his death?\n\nPatient: No, it wasn't before. It really just started about nine months ago, right in that period after he passed away and I started sorting through his records and his messages to get the estate settled. Before that, everything was fine. \n\nBut going through his stuff... man, he had this ancient Dell desktop. Practically a dinosaur, still running Windows 7. I had to physically pull the hard drive out just to extract the files safely without the whole system crashing. I build my own PCs, so it wasn't a big deal to do it. I actually just upgraded my main rig at home with a new graphics card a few weeks ago. The prices finally came down after that whole crypto-mining craze crashed. I still don't understand why people were spending real cash on imaginary internet money anyway. My buddy Mark lost like five thousand dollars on some coin named after a dog. He’s terrible with money, though. He bought a speedboat last year and he doesn't even live near a lake. He has to tow it two hours just to put it in the water, and his truck gets awful gas mileage. I think he drives a Dodge RAM. Do you drive a truck?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any beliefs that others found strange or implausible, such as thinking that you were being communicated with through hidden messages in everyday events or objects?\n\nPatient: No, definitely not. I don't think the TV is talking to me or that the radio is playing secret messages or anything crazy like that. I just look at the actual data regarding my dad, like I said. I'm a systems analyst, I deal with facts. \n\nSpeaking of facts, did you know that the radio station they play in the lobby here is actually owned by a massive conglomerate that owns like, 80% of all local stations in the country? I was reading about media consolidation the other day. It’s wild how few companies control everything we hear. It kind of ruins the whole idea of a \"local DJ,\" you know? They just syndicate the same morning show everywhere. I used to listen to this great morning show when I was in college, these two guys who would just riff for hours. College was fun, but man, the dorms were terrible. The heating in my building was always broken, so in the winter we’d just sit around in our parkas. One time my roommate tried to use a toaster oven to heat the room and blew the fuse for the entire floor. We had to study by flashlight for two days because maintenance was so backed up. Have you ever had to deal with a blown fuse in an old building? It's a nightmare.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs that others found strange or hard to believe, even in areas of your life unrelated to your father’s death or work issues, like thinking something was secretly following you or that certain events were specifically meant for you?\n\nPatient: No, nothing like that. I don't think anyone's following me or sending me secret signs. Like I said, I just look at the facts in front of me. I'm not one of those people who thinks the universe is, like, leaving breadcrumbs for them. \n\nThough speaking of breadcrumbs, I went to this park over the weekend to get some fresh air, just to clear my head, you know? And there was this guy feeding the ducks, but he wasn't using bread—he was using actual duck pellets. Which is great, because bread is actually terrible for ducks. It causes this thing called \"angel wing\" where their wings grow deformed and they can't fly. I watched a whole documentary on it a few years ago. It's crazy how something that seems so harmless, like throwing a piece of sourdough into a pond, can permanently mess up an animal. Sourdough is my favorite bread, though. I tried to get into baking it during the pandemic like everyone else, but my starter died after a week. It just smelled like old gym socks. I ended up just buying it from the bakery down the street. They make this amazing jalapeño cheddar loaf. Do you like spicy food?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something strongly that others told you wasn't true, even when you weren't investigating your father's death or dealing with work problems?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I mean, people tell me I'm wrong about things sometimes, but that's usually just normal disagreements. Like, I don't have any bizarre beliefs or anything. I just stick to what I can prove, you know? \n\nLike at work, I had this massive argument with our database admin last month because he swore we didn't need to upgrade our server redundancy. I told him he was crazy, and sure enough, two weeks later, the primary server went down during a routine patch. We were offline for four hours. Four hours in e-commerce is an eternity. We probably lost thousands in sales just because he didn't want to authorize the budget for the backup cluster. It’s so frustrating when people won't just look at the data. I ended up having to work through my entire weekend just to stabilize the system and write up the post-mortem report. I didn't even get to watch the football game on Sunday. Did you catch the game? My team got absolutely crushed in the fourth quarter. Their defense just fell apart completely. I don't know why they keep running that zone coverage when it clearly isn't working. It's like the coach is just stubborn. Have you ever played fantasy football? I tried it one year, but keeping track of all the stats just felt too much like my actual job.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been absolutely certain about something that others showed you was not true, yet you still believed it, even for a time?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, if you're talking about my dad's situation, then yeah. People tell me I'm wrong about that all the time. I know everyone keeps telling me the hospital records and those donation emails all line up perfectly. But when I actually compare the timestamps, and I look at the missing attachments, and I see the way certain community members just completely avoid my questions when I bring it up... it’s obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened around his death. They can show me all the \"proof\" they want, but the data I'm looking at tells a different story. \n\nIt's kind of like when you look at a spreadsheet and you just know a formula is broken, even if the final number looks okay at first glance. I spend half my day fixing broken formulas. Just yesterday, I was reviewing this massive quarterly report for the marketing team, and they had hardcoded a bunch of values instead of referencing the raw data sheet. Hardcoding! It's like the cardinal sin of data management. If the underlying data changes, your whole report is garbage and you won't even know it. I had to spend three hours rebuilding their entire workbook from scratch. And of course, they wanted it done by 5 PM. I ended up staying late and missing my dinner reservation. I was supposed to go to this new sushi place downtown that just opened up. Have you heard of it? It's supposed to have this amazing omakase menu where the chef just picks everything for you. I love sushi, but I'm always a little paranoid about the quality of the fish if it's not a place I know. You hear those horror stories about parasites and stuff. Do you eat sushi?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a belief that you knew was absolutely true at the time, but later found out was completely false — and if so, how did you come to realize it was false?\n\nPatient: No, I can't really think of a time that happened. I mean, if I believe something is absolutely true, it's because I've already done the legwork and verified it. I don't just jump to conclusions and then have to backtrack later. I look at the data first. \n\nI actually verify everything, even stupid little things. Like, I don't even trust online reviews anymore. A few months ago, I wrote a little script to scrape Amazon reviews and filter out the fake ones based on keyword repetition and posting frequency. It's actually pretty wild how many bots are out there just flooding product pages. I used it last week when I was trying to buy a new mechanical keyboard. The switches on my old one were starting to stick—especially the spacebar, which just drives me completely insane when I'm writing queries. I ended up going with these tactile switches because they have a nice bump to them but aren't too loud. My last roommate had these clicky switches, and it sounded like a literal machine gun every time he typed an email. We actually got into a huge fight about it once because he was typing at 2 AM while I was trying to sleep before a big migration project. He was always staying up late playing some MMO. \n\nAre you a fast typist? I think I average around 90 words a minute, but I've been reading about the Dvorak keyboard layout. Some people swear it's way more efficient than standard QWERTY, but rewiring your brain to learn a new layout just seems exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been absolutely certain about something that was clearly false, and even after others showed you the truth, it took you a long time to change your mind or you still couldn’t let go of that belief?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, if someone shows me actual proof that I'm wrong about something, I'll admit it. But that really doesn't happen, because like I said, I check the data before I make up my mind. I'm not stubborn, I'm just thorough. \n\nIt's kind of like when people try to tell me my car needs premium gas. I drive a 2015 Honda Civic. The manual specifically says regular unleaded is fine. But I took it to this new mechanic last month for an oil change, and he kept insisting that putting premium in it would clean the engine and give me better mileage. He was so pushy about it. I actually pulled out my phone right there in the shop and showed him the compression ratio specs for my engine model to prove that higher octane fuel wouldn't do a single thing except drain my wallet. He got really quiet after that. \n\nMechanics are always trying to upsell you, though. It’s like when you buy electronics and they push those extended warranties. I never buy those. If a hard drive is going to fail, it usually follows the bathtub curve—it either fails in the first month because of a manufacturing defect, or it lasts for years until it just wears out. Have you ever lost data because a drive crashed? It’s the worst feeling. That’s why I have a 3-2-1 backup strategy at home. Three copies of my data, on two different media types, with one offsite. I actually use a cloud service for the offsite part, but I encrypt everything locally before it uploads because you really can't trust those companies with your privacy. Have you read their terms of service? They basically own anything you put on their servers.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any experiences where you strongly believed something about the world that wasn't based on facts or evidence, even if it was just for a short time, such as thinking you had a special ability or a unique connection to a famous person or event?\n\nPatient: No, definitely not. I don't believe in magic or special powers or anything like that. Everything has an explanation if you look hard enough. Like, I don't think I'm secretly related to the president or have some psychic connection to the stock market. Though, honestly, sometimes I wish I did have a connection to the stock market. \n\nI've been trying to get into day trading lately, just as a side thing, you know? But the algorithms the big hedge funds use are just insane. They execute trades in fractions of a millisecond. I was reading this article about how some firms actually bought real estate right next to the stock exchange servers just to shave like, point-zero-zero-one seconds off their ping time. It's crazy what people will do for an edge. I tried writing a basic Python bot to track moving averages on a few tech stocks, but the API limits on the free tier of the data provider were so restrictive it was basically useless. You really have to pay to play. Do you invest much? I feel like real estate is probably safer than stocks right now, but the interest rates are just brutal. My sister just bought a house out in the suburbs and her mortgage is basically double what it would have been three years ago. She has this huge backyard now, though, which is nice for her dog. It's a golden retriever mix, super energetic. I'm more of a cat person myself, they're just so much lower maintenance when you work long hours.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. No, definitely not. Like I said before, I don't just make things up or cling to things that aren't real. If it has no basis in reality, I'm not going to believe it. I need hard evidence for things. 

Although, speaking of things having no basis in reality, it is getting a lot harder to tell what's actually real online these days. Have you seen how advanced deepfakes are getting? The generative adversarial networks they use now are just incredible. I was messing around with an open-source AI image generator last weekend, mostly just trying to see how it handles rendering hands, because that used to be the dead giveaway, right? The AI would always give people like six or seven fingers, or they'd look like they were melting. But they've mostly patched that out in the newer models. 

My aunt actually fell for an AI-generated picture on Facebook last week. She thought they found a giant squid the size of a cruise ship washed up on a beach in Oregon. I had to call her and explain how the lighting artifacts and the shadows on the sand gave it away. She's always on Facebook sharing the most ridiculous stuff. And those weird recipes! Last Thanksgiving she brought this lime green Jell-O salad with shredded carrots and pineapple in it. Shredded carrots in Jell-O! It's an absolute texture nightmare. I ended up just eating the dinner rolls to be polite. Do you cook much? I usually just meal-prep on Sundays so I don't have to think about it during the work week. Mostly just chicken, broccoli, and rice. It gets a little boring, but it's efficient, and it saves me from ordering takeout when I'm stuck debugging a server issue at dinnertime.

I mean, if someone shows me actual proof that I'm wrong about something, I'll admit it. But that really doesn't happen, because like I said, I check the data before I make up my mind. I'm not stubborn, I'm just thorough. \n\nIt's kind of like when people try to tell me my car needs premium gas. I drive a 2015 Honda Civic. The manual specifically says regular unleaded is fine. He got really quiet after that. \n\nMechanics are always trying to upsell you, though. It’s like when you buy electronics and they push those extended warranties. I never buy those. If a hard drive is going to fail, it usually follows the bathtub curve—it either fails in the first month because of a manufacturing defect, or it lasts for years until it just wears out. Have you ever lost data because a drive crashed? It’s the worst feeling. That’s why I have a 3-2-1 backup strategy at home. Three copies of my data, on two different media types, with one offsite. I actually use a cloud service for the offsite part, but I encrypt everything locally before it uploads because you really can't trust those companies with your privacy. Have you read their terms of service? They basically own anything you put on their servers.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any experiences where you strongly believed something about the world that wasn't based on facts or evidence, even if it was just for a short time, such as thinking you had a special ability or a unique connection to a famous person or event?\n\nPatient: No, definitely not. Well, I mean... everyone keeps telling me that the hospital records and those donation emails all line up perfectly. My sister, my mom, the doctors—they all say it's just a tragic coincidence. But they aren't looking at the data the way I am. When I compare the timestamps on the emails, and I see the missing attachments that were supposedly there, and then I notice the way certain community members just completely avoid my questions when I bring it up... it's obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened around my father's death. It's all right there in the metadata if you know how to look for it. 

Metadata is actually fascinating, you know? Most people don't realize how much of a footprint they leave just by sending an email or taking a picture. Like EXIF data on photos. You can pull GPS coordinates, the exact model of the camera, the lens aperture—everything. I wrote a script once to strip all the EXIF data from my family's photos before we uploaded them to a shared drive, just as a security measure. You can't be too careful with location data. 

I actually got into an argument with my sister about that because she loves using those location-sharing apps so she and her friends can see where they all are on a map. I tried to explain to her that she's basically broadcasting her daily routine to whatever third-party data broker buys that app's server logs. But she just rolled her eyes at me. She said I was being paranoid. It's not paranoia if it's in their privacy policy, right? It's like those smart speakers people put in their living rooms. Why would you pay a tech company to put a hot microphone in your house? I only use hardwired connections at home for anything sensitive. Wi-Fi is just too vulnerable to packet sniffing. Do you use a VPN when you're on public Wi-Fi? You really should, especially at coffee shops.

I feel like we're just going in circles here. Yes, absolutely without a doubt. Like I just said, I know everyone keeps telling me that the hospital records and the donation emails line up perfectly. It just started clicking one day. The "click of death," they call it. I lost like three years of project files. I actually tried to take the casing apart in my dorm room to see if the read/write head was stuck on the platter. Huge mistake. You need a certified clean room for that because even a single speck of dust can completely destroy the data. 

Dust is everywhere, too. Have you ever looked at a sunbeam coming through your window and just seen all the particles floating there? It's mostly dead skin cells, which is incredibly gross if you think about it. I actually bought a HEPA air purifier for my bedroom last month because my allergies were acting up. It has this little LED indicator ring that turns red when the air quality is bad, and it goes absolutely crazy every time I cook on the stove. I'm pretty sure my stove's exhaust hood is just venting straight into the ceiling cavity instead of outside. I really need to get my landlord to look at that, but he's impossible to get a hold of. He always sends his son to do the repairs instead, and the kid barely knows which end of a screwdriver to hold. Last time he was here, he tracked mud all over my hallway rug.

My team got absolutely crushed in the fourth quarter. Their defense just fell apart completely. I don't know why they keep running that zone coverage when it clearly isn't working. It's like the coach is just stubborn. Have you ever played fantasy football? I tried it one year, but keeping track of all the stats just felt too much like my actual job.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been absolutely certain about something that others showed you was not true, yet you still believed it, even for a time?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, if you're talking about my dad's situation, then yeah. People tell me I'm wrong about that all the time. I know everyone keeps telling me the hospital records and those donation emails all line up perfectly. But when I actually compare the timestamps, and I look at the missing attachments, and I see the way certain community members just completely avoid my questions when I bring it up... it’s obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened around his death. Just yesterday, I was reviewing this massive quarterly report for the marketing team, and they had hardcoded a bunch of values instead of referencing the raw data sheet. Hardcoding! It's like the cardinal sin of data management. If the underlying data changes, your whole report is garbage and you won't even know it. I had to spend three hours rebuilding their entire workbook from scratch. And of course, they wanted it done by 5 PM. I ended up staying late and missing my dinner reservation. Do you eat sushi?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a belief that you knew was absolutely true at the time, but later found out was completely false — and if so, how did you come to realize it was false?\n\nPatient: No, I can't really think of a time that happened. I look at the data first. \n\nI actually verify everything, even stupid little things. Like, I don't even trust online reviews anymore. I'm not stubborn, I'm just thorough. \n\nIt's kind of like when people try to tell me my car needs premium gas. I drive a 2015 Honda Civic. The manual specifically says regular unleaded is fine. Have you ever lost data because a drive crashed? It’s the worst feeling. That’s why I have a 3-2-1 backup strategy at home. Three copies of my data, on two different media types, with one offsite. I actually use a cloud service for the offsite part, but I encrypt everything locally before it uploads because you really can't trust those companies with your privacy. Have you read their terms of service? They basically own anything you put on their servers.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any experiences where you strongly believed something about the world that wasn't based on facts or evidence, even if it was just for a short time, such as thinking you had a special ability or a unique connection to a famous person or event?\n\nPatient: No, definitely not. Some people swear it's way more efficient than standard QWERTY, but rewiring your brain to learn a new layout just seems exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been absolutely certain about something that was clearly false, and even after others showed you the truth, it took you a long time to change your mind or you still couldn’t let go of that belief?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, if someone shows me actual proof that I'm wrong about something, I'll admit it. But that really doesn't happen, because like I said, I check the data before I make up my mind. I'm not stubborn, I'm just thorough. \n\nIt's kind of like when people try to tell me my car needs premium gas. I drive a 2015 Honda Civic. The manual specifically says regular unleaded is fine. But I took it to this new mechanic last month for an oil change, and he kept insisting that putting premium in it would clean the engine and give me better mileage. He was so pushy about it. I actually pulled out my phone right there in the shop and showed him the compression ratio specs for my engine model to prove that higher octane fuel wouldn't do a single thing except drain my wallet. He got really quiet after that. \n\nMechanics are always trying to upsell you, though. It’s like when you buy electronics and they push those extended warranties. I never buy those. If a hard drive is going to fail, it usually follows the bathtub curve—it either fails in the first month because of a manufacturing defect, or it lasts for years until it just wears out. Have you ever lost data because a drive crashed? It’s the worst feeling. That’s why I have a 3-2-1 backup strategy at home. Three copies of my data, on two different media types, with one offsite. I actually use a cloud service for the offsite part, but I encrypt everything locally before it uploads because you really can't trust those companies with your privacy. Have you read their terms of service? They basically own anything you put on their servers.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any experiences where you strongly believed something about the world that wasn't based on facts or evidence, even if it was just for a short time, such as thinking you had a special ability or a unique connection to a famous person or event?\n\nPatient: No, definitely not. I don't believe in magic or special powers or anything like that. Everything has an explanation if you look hard enough. Like, I don't think I'm secretly related to the president or have some psychic connection to the stock market. Though, honestly, sometimes I wish I did have a connection to the stock market. \n\nI've been trying to get into day trading lately, just as a side thing, you know? But the algorithms the big hedge funds use are just insane. They execute trades in fractions of a millisecond. I was reading this article about how some firms actually bought real estate right next to the stock exchange servers just to shave like, point-zero-zero-one seconds off their ping time. It's crazy what people will do for an edge. I tried writing a basic Python bot to track moving averages on a few tech stocks, but the API limits on the free tier of the data provider were so restrictive it was basically useless. You really have to pay to play. Do you invest much? I feel like real estate is probably safer than stocks right now, but the interest rates are just brutal. My sister just bought a house out in the suburbs and her mortgage is basically double what it would have been three years ago. She has this huge backyard now, though, which is nice for her dog. It's a golden retriever mix, super energetic. I'm more of a cat person myself, they're just so much lower maintenance when you work long hours.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something strongly that had absolutely no basis in reality and wasn't related to your father’s death or work issues, and even after others showed you the truth, you still couldn’t let go of that belief?\n\nPatient: No, definitely not. Like I said before, I don't just make things up or cling to things that aren't real. If it has no basis in reality, I'm not going to believe it. I need hard evidence for things. \n\nAlthough, speaking of things having no basis in reality, it is getting a lot harder to tell what's actually real online these days. Have you seen how advanced deepfakes are getting? The generative adversarial networks they use now are just incredible. I was messing around with an open-source AI image generator last weekend, mostly just trying to see how it handles rendering hands, because that used to be the dead giveaway, right? The AI would always give people like six or seven fingers, or they'd look like they were melting. But they've mostly patched that out in the newer models. \n\nMy aunt actually fell for an AI-generated picture on Facebook last week. She thought they found a giant squid the size of a cruise ship washed up on a beach in Oregon. I had to call her and explain how the lighting artifacts and the shadows on the sand gave it away. She's always on Facebook sharing the most ridiculous stuff. And those weird recipes! Last Thanksgiving she brought this lime green Jell-O salad with shredded carrots and pineapple in it. Shredded carrots in Jell-O! It's an absolute texture nightmare. I ended up just eating the dinner rolls to be polite. Do you cook much? I usually just meal-prep on Sundays so I don't have to think about it during the work week. Mostly just chicken, broccoli, and rice. It gets a little boring, but it's efficient, and it saves me from ordering takeout when I'm stuck debugging a server issue at dinnertime.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a belief that you were absolutely certain was true, but others told you was not real, and despite their attempts to convince you otherwise, you still believed it strongly, even for a long time?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... everyone keeps telling me that the hospital records and those donation emails all line up perfectly. My sister, my mom, the doctors—they all say it's just a tragic coincidence. But they aren't looking at the data the way I am. When I compare the timestamps on the emails, and I see the missing attachments that were supposedly there, and then I notice the way certain community members just completely avoid my questions when I bring it up... it's obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened around my father's death. It's all right there in the metadata if you know how to look for it. \n\nMetadata is actually fascinating, you know? Most people don't realize how much of a footprint they leave just by sending an email or taking a picture. Like EXIF data on photos. You can pull GPS coordinates, the exact model of the camera, the lens aperture—everything. I wrote a script once to strip all the EXIF data from my family's photos before we uploaded them to a shared drive, just as a security measure. You can't be too careful with location data. \n\nI actually got into an argument with my sister about that because she loves using those location-sharing apps so she and her friends can see where they all are on a map. I tried to explain to her that she's basically broadcasting her daily routine to whatever third-party data broker buys that app's server logs. But she just rolled her eyes at me. She said I was being paranoid. It's not paranoia if it's in their privacy policy, right? It's like those smart speakers people put in their living rooms. Why would you pay a tech company to put a hot microphone in your house? I only use hardwired connections at home for anything sensitive. Wi-Fi is just too vulnerable to packet sniffing. Do you use a VPN when you're on public Wi-Fi? You really should, especially at coffee shops.\n\nDoctor: Even though others have shown you hospital records and emails that contradict your belief about your father's death, do you still hold onto your belief without any doubt?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, I absolutely still believe it. Because I'm looking at the raw data, and they're just looking at the surface. I know everyone keeps telling me the hospital records and those donation emails all line up perfectly, but they don't. When I actually compare the timestamps, and I see those missing attachments, and then I factor in the way certain community members just completely dodge my questions... it’s completely obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death. \n\nIt's like trying to debug a system where someone has deliberately altered the log files. You have to look for the gaps, you know? The things that aren't there. Speaking of gaps, I was actually looking at the server uptime logs for my personal website yesterday, and I noticed this weird three-minute drop at like 4:00 AM on a Tuesday. Usually, my hosting provider is pretty solid, they guarantee 99.9% uptime, but three minutes is an eternity in server time. I submitted a support ticket, and they tried to tell me it was just routine node maintenance. But they didn't post any scheduled maintenance notices on their status page beforehand. It just felt really unprofessional. \n\nI've been thinking about just migrating the whole thing to a dedicated server I run myself from my apartment. The cooling would be a nightmare, though. My apartment gets so hot in the summer anyway, the AC unit in the window barely keeps up. Last July, it got up to 90 degrees in my living room, and my cat just laid flat on the tile floor in the bathroom for two days straight. Do you have pets? Cats are really sensitive to heat. I had to put ice cubes in his water bowl just to make sure he was staying hydrated. But yeah, you can't just trust what people tell you when the data says something else.\n\nDoctor: Even though others have shown you hospital records and emails that contradict your belief about your father's death, do you still hold onto your belief without any doubt?\n\nPatient: I feel like we're just going in circles here. Yes, absolutely without a doubt. Like I just said, I know everyone keeps telling me that the hospital records and the donation emails line up perfectly. But they aren't looking at the actual evidence. When I sit down and compare the timestamps, and I see those missing attachments, and I watch how certain community members just completely avoid my questions... it’s obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened with my father’s death. \n\nAnd honestly, speaking of missing attachments, it always reminds me of this massive data loss I dealt with back in college. I had this old spinning hard drive, a Seagate, I think. It just started clicking one day. The \"click of death,\" they call it. I lost like three years of project files. I actually tried to take the casing apart in my dorm room to see if the read/write head was stuck on the platter. Huge mistake. You need a certified clean room for that because even a single speck of dust can completely destroy the data. \n\nDust is everywhere, too. Have you ever looked at a sunbeam coming through your window and just seen all the particles floating there? It's mostly dead skin cells, which is incredibly gross if you think about it. I actually bought a HEPA air purifier for my bedroom last month because my allergies were acting up. It has this little LED indicator ring that turns red when the air quality is bad, and it goes absolutely crazy every time I cook on the stove. I'm pretty sure my stove's exhaust hood is just venting straight into the ceiling cavity instead of outside. I really need to get my landlord to look at that, but he's impossible to get a hold of. He always sends his son to do the repairs instead, and the kid barely knows which end of a screwdriver to hold. Last time he was here, he tracked mud all over my hallway rug."
